Nice to meet you too. Um, I guess I'm here because... well, I just keep feeling like I ruined things for everybody lately. Like I'm a total fake in my apprenticeship. I'm training to be a pipefitter, and I just feel like I let my family down, and even the younger apprentices, over stuff I know other people would probably just fix and move on from. 

Like cutting a piece of pipe a quarter-inch too short, you know? Which, actually, reminds me, I lost my favorite pipe cutter the other day. It was this really nice Milwaukee one, bright red. I think I left it on the bumper of my truck when I stopped at this gas station on Route 9. The guy who works the counter there, Gary, he always has these crazy stories about his dogs. He's got three golden retrievers and they just completely destroy his yard. I mean, dogs are great, I grew up with a beagle myself, but three big ones? That's a lot of dog hair. I don't know how his wife deals with it, honestly. 

But um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just feel like a fraud at work and like I'm letting everyone down.

Um, I don't really think so? Like, I wouldn't say I'm depressed most days. I'm just... I don't know, maybe just drained. Um, easily distracted? I don't... I don't think they said that. I mean, I got decent grades in school, mostly. I really liked history class, especially when we learned about the Industrial Revolution. All those massive steam engines and factories being built, it's just fascinating how quickly everything changed. My teacher for that class, Mr. Henderson, he had this incredible collection of antique pocket watches. He'd bring them in sometimes to show us how the gears worked. I actually bought a cheap replica pocket watch at a flea market a couple of years ago because of him. The flea market was huge, out in this big dusty field near the county line. They had everything there—old furniture, weird taxidermy, even a guy selling homemade hot sauce out of the trunk of his Buick. I tried a sample of the hottest one, and I swear I couldn't feel my tongue for an hour. I had to buy a giant lemon slushie just to cool my mouth down. The slushie was so cold it gave me a brain freeze, you know that sharp pain right behind your eyes? It was brutal. But um... yeah, I don't remember anyone saying I was distracted in school.

not really? We used that TV until the screen literally just went bright green one day, right in the middle of a hockey game. The Bruins were tied in the third period, too. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, a stressful event? I mean, I guess it started about five months ago, right after I took on this sustainable plumbing certification course. I was already doing my apprenticeship, so adding the classes on top of that was a lot. The classes are actually really interesting, though. We're learning all about greywater systems and how to reuse water from sinks and showers to flush toilets and water gardens. It's wild how much clean water we just flush down the drain every single day. My instructor for the course, she's really passionate about it. She actually lives in this tiny house she built herself, completely off the grid. She has solar panels and a composting toilet, which, honestly, I don't know if I could ever get used to. I watched this whole documentary on tiny houses last week because of her. Some of them are built on trailers so you can just hook them up to a truck and drive away whenever you want. That sounds kind of nice, just being able to pack up your whole house and move to the mountains or something. I've always wanted to visit the Rockies. I saw a picture of Banff National Park once and the water in the lakes there is this insane, bright turquoise color because of the rock flour from the glaciers. It doesn't even look real. But um, yeah, so I guess the whole thing with my mood and energy, it's been going on for about five months now, since I started that extra certification.

Yeah, I just feel like a fraud at work and like I'm letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really think so? Like, I wouldn't say I'm depressed most days. I'm just... I don't know, maybe just drained. I've actually been staying up way too late watching this documentary series about deep-sea welders. It is wild what those guys do underwater. I actually thought about getting into underwater welding when I first started my associate's degree, but honestly, deep water kind of freaks me out. My uncle used to have this little aluminum fishing boat, and we took it out on the lake once, and he caught this massive catfish. It was like half my size when I was seven. Those whiskers were just thrashing everywhere on the deck, it was completely terrifying. I wouldn't even go near the cooler for the rest of the weekend. But... wait, sorry, what did you just ask me? Something about being sad?\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need, like tools, keys, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Um, not really? I mean, I lost that pipe cutter, but I usually keep pretty good track of my stuff. My phone's basically glued to my hand anyway because I've been texting my sister a lot. She just got engaged last weekend, actually. It was this whole surprise thing at a vineyard, which is crazy because she doesn't even really drink wine. She's more of a hard cider person. We used to go apple picking every fall when we were kids, and my mom would make these huge batches of homemade cider on the stove. It made the whole house smell like cinnamon and cloves for days. I tried making it myself last year in my crockpot, but I definitely messed up the spices because it tasted like straight-up potpourri. I ended up pouring the whole batch down the sink, which was a bummer because I bought all these expensive honeycrisp apples for it. But yeah, no, I don't really lose my keys or anything like that. Just the pipe cutter.\n\nDoctor: When you were a kid in school, did teachers or your parents often say you were easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Um, easily distracted? I don't... I don't think they said that. I mean, I got decent grades in school, mostly. I really liked history class, especially when we learned about the Industrial Revolution. All those massive steam engines and factories being built, it's just fascinating how quickly everything changed. My teacher for that class, Mr. Henderson, he had this incredible collection of antique pocket watches. He'd bring them in sometimes to show us how the gears worked. I actually bought a cheap replica pocket watch at a flea market a couple of years ago because of him. The flea market was huge, out in this big dusty field near the county line. They had everything there—old furniture, weird taxidermy, even a guy selling homemade hot sauce out of the trunk of his Buick. I tried a sample of the hottest one, and I swear I couldn't feel my tongue for an hour. I had to buy a giant lemon slushie just to cool my mouth down. The slushie was so cold it gave me a brain freeze, you know that sharp pain right behind your eyes? It was brutal. But um... yeah, I don't remember anyone saying I was distracted in school.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on what someone is saying to you, even when you’re trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Trouble focusing? Um, no, not really. I'm usually pretty good at listening. Like on the job, you have to listen really closely when you're roughing in pipes or you'll mess up the whole layout. My journeyman, Greg, he's always giving me these super detailed instructions. He has this really deep, rumbly voice, kind of like a radio announcer? Actually, he told me he used to do voiceovers for local car dealership commercials back in the day. You know those ones where they just yell about Sunday blowout sales? My dad used to absolutely hate those commercials. He'd mute the TV every single time one came on. We had this old boxy TV in the living room that weighed like two hundred pounds, and the remote was completely held together by black electrical tape. We used that TV until the screen literally just went bright green one day, right in the middle of a hockey game. The Bruins were tied in the third period, too. My dad was so mad he actually threw a couch pillow at it and knocked over his coffee on the rug. It left this huge stain that never came out. But um... yeah, no, I don't really zone out when people are talking to me. I pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Um, a stressful event? I mean, I guess it started about five months ago, right after I took on this sustainable plumbing certification course. I was already doing my apprenticeship, so adding the classes on top of that was a lot. The classes are actually really interesting, though. We're learning all about greywater systems and how to reuse water from sinks and showers to flush toilets and water gardens. It's wild how much clean water we just flush down the drain every single day. My instructor for the course, she's really passionate about it. She actually lives in this tiny house she built herself, completely off the grid. She has solar panels and a composting toilet, which, honestly, I don't know if I could ever get used to. I watched this whole documentary on tiny houses last week because of her. Some of them are built on trailers so you can just hook them up to a truck and drive away whenever you want. That sounds kind of nice, just being able to pack up your whole house and move to the mountains or something. I've always wanted to visit the Rockies. I saw a picture of Banff National Park once and the water in the lakes there is this insane, bright turquoise color because of the rock flour from the glaciers. It doesn't even look real. But um, yeah, so I guess the whole thing with my mood and energy, it's been going on for about five months now, since I started that extra certification.\n\nDoctor: If you were to stop the extra certification course, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Um, if I stopped the course? I don't know, maybe? I mean, I can't really just drop it now, I've already paid the tuition and bought all the textbooks. Have you seen how expensive textbooks are lately? It's ridiculous. I had to buy this one massive manual on advanced pipefitting techniques, and it cost like two hundred dollars. It's so heavy, I actually use it to prop open the door to my apartment when I'm bringing in groceries. Um, yeah, actually. It's getting kind of bad. I tried a sample of the hottest one, and I swear I couldn't feel my tongue for an hour. I had to buy a giant lemon slushie just to cool my mouth down. The slushie was so cold it gave me a brain freeze, you know that sharp pain right behind your eyes? It was brutal. But um... yeah, I don't remember anyone saying I was distracted in school.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on what someone is saying to you, even when you’re trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Trouble focusing? Um, no, not really. I'm usually pretty good at listening. Like on the job, you have to listen really closely when you're roughing in pipes or you'll mess up the whole layout. My journeyman, Greg, he's always giving me these super detailed instructions. He has this really deep, rumbly voice, kind of like a radio announcer? Actually, he told me he used to do voiceovers for local car dealership commercials back in the day. You know those ones where they just yell about Sunday blowout sales? My dad used to absolutely hate those commercials. He'd mute the TV every single time one came on. We had this old boxy TV in the living room that weighed like two hundred pounds, and the remote was completely held together by black electrical tape. We used that TV until the screen literally just went bright green one day, right in the middle of a hockey game. The Bruins were tied in the third period, too. My dad was so mad he actually threw a couch pillow at it and knocked over his coffee on the rug. It left this huge stain that never came out. But um... yeah, no, I don't really zone out when people are talking to me. I pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Um, a stressful event? I mean, I guess it started about five months ago, right after I took on this sustainable plumbing certification course. I was already doing my apprenticeship, so adding the classes on top of that was a lot. The classes are actually really interesting, though. We're learning all about greywater systems and how to reuse water from sinks and showers to flush toilets and water gardens. It's wild how much clean water we just flush down the drain every single day. My instructor for the course, she's really passionate about it. She actually lives in this tiny house she built herself, completely off the grid. She has solar panels and a composting toilet, which, honestly, I don't know if I could ever get used to. I watched this whole documentary on tiny houses last week because of her. Some of them are built on trailers so you can just hook them up to a truck and drive away whenever you want. That sounds kind of nice, just being able to pack up your whole house and move to the mountains or something. I've always wanted to visit the Rockies. I saw a picture of Banff National Park once and the water in the lakes there is this insane, bright turquoise color because of the rock flour from the glaciers. It doesn't even look real. But um, yeah, so I guess the whole thing with my mood and energy, it's been going on for about five months now, since I started that extra certification.\n\nDoctor: If you were to stop the extra certification course, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Um, if I stopped the course? I don't know, maybe? I mean, I can't really just drop it now, I've already paid the tuition and bought all the textbooks. Have you seen how expensive textbooks are lately? It's ridiculous. I had to buy this one massive manual on advanced pipefitting techniques, and it cost like two hundred dollars. It's so heavy, I actually use it to prop open the door to my apartment when I'm bringing in groceries. My apartment building is so old, the doors are solid wood and they swing shut so fast if you don't wedge something in there. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got her hand caught in the lobby door once because she wasn't paying attention. She's this really sweet older lady who always smells like lavender and bakes these amazing snickerdoodle cookies. She brings me a plate of them every Christmas. Last year she put them in this really pretty vintage tin that had a picture of a snowy village on it. I actually kept the tin, I use it to store all my extra drill bits and screws now. It's perfectly sized for that. But um... yeah, I'm not sure if quitting the class would make a difference. I haven't really thought about stopping.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings started to interfere with your work or classes?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, actually. It's getting kind of bad. My instructors and my foreman, Greg—the guy with the radio voice I mentioned?—they've both had to chase me down lately because I'm leaving sketches unfinished and my work is just... slower than it should be. I'm really scared I'm going to mess up this apprenticeship and the certification, and I worked so hard to get here. It took me forever just to find a company that would take on a female apprentice, honestly. A lot of the older guys in the union are still pretty old-school about that stuff. My uncle is in the union, actually, he's an electrician, and he told me I'd have to work twice as hard to prove myself. He's a really great guy, though. He helped me rewire my entire kitchen when I moved into my apartment because the outlets were completely fried. We found these old newspapers stuffed in the walls for insulation from like, 1952. It was crazy, there was this huge ad for a brand new refrigerator that only cost like a hundred bucks. Can you imagine buying a fridge for a hundred dollars now? You can barely get a toaster for that. I bought a toaster last week and it has this weird setting just for bagels, but it always burns the edges anyway. I love everything bagels, though, especially with that jalapeño cream cheese from the deli down the street. I'm really scared I'm going to mess up this apprenticeship and the certification, and I worked so hard to get here. It took me forever just to find a company that would take on a female apprentice, honestly. A lot of the older guys in the union are still pretty old-school about that stuff. My uncle is in the union, actually, he's an electrician, and he told me I'd have to work twice as hard to prove myself. He's a really great guy, though. He helped me rewire my entire kitchen when I moved into my apartment because the outlets were completely fried. We found these old newspapers stuffed in the walls for insulation from like, 1952. It was crazy, there was this huge ad for a brand new refrigerator that only cost like a hundred bucks. Can you imagine buying a fridge for a hundred dollars now? You can barely get a toaster for that. I bought a toaster last week and it has this weird setting just for bagels, but it always burns the edges anyway. I love everything bagels, though, especially with that jalapeño cream cheese from the deli down the street. But um... yeah, so my foreman and instructors are definitely noticing that I'm falling behind, and it's really stressing me out.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just work or the certification course?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, worrying about other things? I don't know, I guess I worry a normal amount? Like, I wouldn't say I'm constantly stressing about everything. Mostly just the apprenticeship right now. Though, I did spend like an hour yesterday worrying about whether I left my straightener plugged in. I have this really bad habit of doing my hair and just running out the door. My straightener gets so hot, too, it's one of those ceramic ones. I bought it at the mall last year from one of those kiosks where they practically chase you down to curl a piece of your hair. The girl who sold it to me had the most amazing pink hair, though. It was like a really bright, neon magenta. I asked her what dye she used, and she said it was this brand you can only buy online. I thought about dyeing my hair pink once, but I figured it would look weird with my hard hat. Plus, the dye stains everything. My friend Sarah dyed her hair blue in high school and her bathtub was permanently Smurf-colored for like three years. Her mom was so mad. They ended up having to re-glaze the whole tub before they sold the house. But um... yeah, no, I don't think I worry about too many other things besides work and school.

S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: For a while I was getting by on three or four hours and still showing up wired for the job and class, but now I either can't fall asleep or I wake before my alarm feeling like I never rested.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Um, yeah, I guess I have. It's weird because I used to love drawing and stuff. But lately I can just walk right past my sketchbook and not even care. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, sad or down? I don't think so. I mean, I'm stressed about the apprenticeship and all, but I wouldn't say I feel sad most days. Just kind of... blank, maybe? Like with the volleyball and the drawing. But not really sad. My mom is always telling me I need to smile more, though. She's got this whole theory that if you just force yourself to smile, your brain gets tricked into being happy. She read it in this magazine she gets at the hair salon. She goes to the same salon every three weeks to get her roots touched up because she's terrified of anyone knowing she's going gray. The stylist there, her name is Brenda, she has these tiny little dogs that just run around the salon all day. They're Chihuahuas, I think, but they look like little rats. One of them actually chewed on my shoelace once when I went in there to drop off my mom's keys. I was wearing my good work boots, too, the steel-toed ones. They take forever to break in, so you really don't want a dog chewing on them. You have to rub mink oil on the leather to soften it up, which smells kind of weird, honestly. It smells like... I don't know, old basement? But um... yeah, no, I don't think I've been feeling sad for five months or anything. Just mostly stressed and tired.

Which, actually, reminds me, I lost my favorite pipe cutter the other day. It was this really nice Milwaukee one, bright red. Like, I wouldn't say I'm depressed most days. I'm just... I don't know, maybe just drained. I've actually been staying up way too late watching this documentary series about deep-sea welders. It is wild what those guys do underwater. I actually thought about getting into underwater welding when I first started my associate's degree, but honestly, deep water kind of freaks me out. My uncle used to have this little aluminum fishing boat, and we took it out on the lake once, and he caught this massive catfish. It was like half my size when I was seven. Those whiskers were just thrashing everywhere on the deck, it was completely terrifying. I wouldn't even go near the cooler for the rest of the weekend. But... wait, sorry, what did you just ask me? I mean, I lost that pipe cutter, but I usually keep pretty good track of my stuff. My phone's basically glued to my hand anyway because I've been texting my sister a lot. She just got engaged last weekend, actually. It was this whole surprise thing at a vineyard, which is crazy because she doesn't even really drink wine. She's more of a hard cider person. We used to go apple picking every fall when we were kids, and my mom would make these huge batches of homemade cider on the stove. It made the whole house smell like cinnamon and cloves for days. I tried making it myself last year in my crockpot, but I definitely messed up the spices because it tasted like straight-up potpourri. I ended up pouring the whole batch down the sink, which was a bummer because I bought all these expensive honeycrisp apples for it. But yeah, no, I don't really lose my keys or anything like that. Just the pipe cutter.\n\nDoctor: When you were a kid in school, did teachers or your parents often say you were easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Um, easily distracted? I don't... I don't think they said that. I mean, I got decent grades in school, mostly. I really liked history class, especially when we learned about the Industrial Revolution. All those massive steam engines and factories being built, it's just fascinating how quickly everything changed. My teacher for that class, Mr. Henderson, he had this incredible collection of antique pocket watches. He'd bring them in sometimes to show us how the gears worked. I actually bought a cheap replica pocket watch at a flea market a couple of years ago because of him. The flea market was huge, out in this big dusty field near the county line. They had everything there—old furniture, weird taxidermy, even a guy selling homemade hot sauce out of the trunk of his Buick. I tried a sample of the hottest one, and I swear I couldn't feel my tongue for an hour. I had to buy a giant lemon slushie just to cool my mouth down. The slushie was so cold it gave me a brain freeze, you know that sharp pain right behind your eyes? It was brutal. But um... yeah, I don't remember anyone saying I was distracted in school.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on what someone is saying to you, even when you’re trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Trouble focusing? Um, no, not really. I'm usually pretty good at listening. Like on the job, you have to listen really closely when you're roughing in pipes or you'll mess up the whole layout. My journeyman, Greg, he's always giving me these super detailed instructions. He has this really deep, rumbly voice, kind of like a radio announcer? Actually, he told me he used to do voiceovers for local car dealership commercials back in the day. You know those ones where they just yell about Sunday blowout sales? My dad used to absolutely hate those commercials. He'd mute the TV every single time one came on. We had this old boxy TV in the living room that weighed like two hundred pounds, and the remote was completely held together by black electrical tape. We used that TV until the screen literally just went bright green one day, right in the middle of a hockey game. The Bruins were tied in the third period, too. My dad was so mad he actually threw a couch pillow at it and knocked over his coffee on the rug. It left this huge stain that never came out. But um... yeah, no, I don't really zone out when people are talking to me. I pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Um, a stressful event? I mean, I guess it started about five months ago, right after I took on this sustainable plumbing certification course. I was already doing my apprenticeship, so adding the classes on top of that was a lot. The classes are actually really interesting, though. We're learning all about greywater systems and how to reuse water from sinks and showers to flush toilets and water gardens. It's wild how much clean water we just flush down the drain every single day. My instructor for the course, she's really passionate about it. She actually lives in this tiny house she built herself, completely off the grid. She has solar panels and a composting toilet, which, honestly, I don't know if I could ever get used to. I watched this whole documentary on tiny houses last week because of her. Some of them are built on trailers so you can just hook them up to a truck and drive away whenever you want. That sounds kind of nice, just being able to pack up your whole house and move to the mountains or something. I've always wanted to visit the Rockies. I saw a picture of Banff National Park once and the water in the lakes there is this insane, bright turquoise color because of the rock flour from the glaciers. It doesn't even look real. But um, yeah, so I guess the whole thing with my mood and energy, it's been going on for about five months now, since I started that extra certification.\n\nDoctor: If you were to stop the extra certification course, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Um, if I stopped the course? I don't know, maybe? I mean, I can't really just drop it now, I've already paid the tuition and bought all the textbooks. Have you seen how expensive textbooks are lately? It's ridiculous. I had to buy this one massive manual on advanced pipefitting techniques, and it cost like two hundred dollars. It's so heavy, I actually use it to prop open the door to my apartment when I'm bringing in groceries. My apartment building is so old, the doors are solid wood and they swing shut so fast if you don't wedge something in there. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got her hand caught in the lobby door once because she wasn't paying attention. She's this really sweet older lady who always smells like lavender and bakes these amazing snickerdoodle cookies. She brings me a plate of them every Christmas. Last year she put them in this really pretty vintage tin that had a picture of a snowy village on it. I actually kept the tin, I use it to store all my extra drill bits and screws now. It's perfectly sized for that. But um... yeah, I'm not sure if quitting the class would make a difference. I haven't really thought about stopping.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings started to interfere with your work or classes?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, actually. It's getting kind of bad. My instructors and my foreman, Greg—the guy with the radio voice I mentioned?—they've both had to chase me down lately because I'm leaving sketches unfinished and my work is just... slower than it should be. I'm really scared I'm going to mess up this apprenticeship and the certification, and I worked so hard to get here. It took me forever just to find a company that would take on a female apprentice, honestly. A lot of the older guys in the union are still pretty old-school about that stuff. My uncle is in the union, actually, he's an electrician, and he told me I'd have to work twice as hard to prove myself. He's a really great guy, though. He helped me rewire my entire kitchen when I moved into my apartment because the outlets were completely fried. We found these old newspapers stuffed in the walls for insulation from like, 1952. It was crazy, there was this huge ad for a brand new refrigerator that only cost like a hundred bucks. Can you imagine buying a fridge for a hundred dollars now? You can barely get a toaster for that. I bought a toaster last week and it has this weird setting just for bagels, but it always burns the edges anyway. I love everything bagels, though, especially with that jalapeño cream cheese from the deli down the street. But um... yeah, so my foreman and instructors are definitely noticing that I'm falling behind, and it's really stressing me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just work or the certification course?\n\nPatient: Um, worrying about other things? I don't know, I guess I worry a normal amount? Like, I wouldn't say I'm constantly stressing about everything. Mostly just the apprenticeship right now. Though, I did spend like an hour yesterday worrying about whether I left my straightener plugged in. I have this really bad habit of doing my hair and just running out the door. My straightener gets so hot, too, it's one of those ceramic ones. I bought it at the mall last year from one of those kiosks where they practically chase you down to curl a piece of your hair. The girl who sold it to me had the most amazing pink hair, though. It was like a really bright, neon magenta. I asked her what dye she used, and she said it was this brand you can only buy online. I thought about dyeing my hair pink once, but I figured it would look weird with my hard hat. Plus, the dye stains everything. My friend Sarah dyed her hair blue in high school and her bathtub was permanently Smurf-colored for like three years. Her mom was so mad. They ended up having to re-glaze the whole tub before they sold the house. But um... yeah, no, I don't think I worry about too many other things besides work and school.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess I have. It's weird because I used to love drawing and stuff. But lately I can just walk right past my sketchbook and not even care. Same with my volleyball league, I've skipped the last few games. I even ignore the group texts for this volunteer thing I do. It's like I don't feel much of anything about them anymore, even though those were the things that made me feel like myself, you know? The volunteer group is actually really cool, we help build community gardens in empty lots around the city. Last month we planted this huge row of heirloom tomatoes in this lot over by the old textile factory. The factory is totally abandoned now, but the brickwork on the outside is amazing. It has these huge arched windows that are all boarded up. Some guy spray-painted this really intricate mural of a dragon on one of the boards. It's actually really good, way better than the usual tagging you see around there. I tried to take a picture of it, but my phone camera is completely scratched up because I dropped it in my toolbox last week right onto a pile of loose screws. I really need to get a new phone case, maybe one of those heavy-duty ones that construction workers use. The guy at the phone store tried to sell me one that looked like a tire tread, but it was so bulky it wouldn't even fit in my pocket. Women's pockets are useless anyway, they barely hold a tube of chapstick. I buy this cherry chapstick that comes in a three-pack, but I always lose two of them within a week. It's a mystery where they go. But um... yeah, I just don't have the energy or interest for my usual stuff right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past five months, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Um, sad or down? I don't think so. I mean, I'm stressed about the apprenticeship and all, but I wouldn't say I feel sad most days. Just kind of... blank, maybe? Like with the volleyball and the drawing. Um, before these last five months? Well, honestly, this whole stretch of just... not caring about anything has been going on for like nine months now. It's just that I could kind of fake it better before, but then school and work started slipping, so it became way harder to hide. It's like when you have a leak behind a wall, you know? You don't see it for a long time until the drywall gets all mushy and the paint starts bubbling. I had to fix a pipe like that last week in this really old apartment building downtown. The walls were plaster and lath, which is such a nightmare to cut through. You need a special saw blade or it just ruins the plaster. The lady who lived there was so nice, though. She kept offering me these little hard candies from a crystal dish on her coffee table. They were those strawberry ones with the wrapper that looks like a strawberry? I haven't seen those since I was a kid. My grandma used to keep them in her purse. Her purse was this massive leather bag that weighed like ten pounds, and she had everything in there. Mints, tissues, old receipts, a tiny sewing kit. Once, she pulled out a whole stapler to fix my cousin's hem at a wedding. It was a beach wedding, too, which was a terrible idea because everyone's heels kept sinking into the sand. But um... yeah, so it's been about nine months of feeling like this, not just five.

I ended up pouring the whole batch down the sink, which was a bummer because I bought all these expensive honeycrisp apples for it. But yeah, no, I don't really lose my keys or anything like that. I don't think they said that. I mean, I got decent grades in school, mostly. I really liked history class, especially when we learned about the Industrial Revolution. My teacher for that class, Mr. Henderson, he had this incredible collection of antique pocket watches. He'd bring them in sometimes to show us how the gears worked. I actually bought a cheap replica pocket watch at a flea market a couple of years ago because of him. The flea market was huge, out in this big dusty field near the county line. They had everything there—old furniture, weird taxidermy, even a guy selling homemade hot sauce out of the trunk of his Buick. I tried a sample of the hottest one, and I swear I couldn't feel my tongue for an hour. I had to buy a giant lemon slushie just to cool my mouth down. We used that TV until the screen literally just went bright green one day, right in the middle of a hockey game. The Bruins were tied in the third period, too. We're learning all about greywater systems and how to reuse water from sinks and showers to flush toilets and water gardens. It's wild how much clean water we just flush down the drain every single day. My instructor for the course, she's really passionate about it. She actually lives in this tiny house she built herself, completely off the grid. She has solar panels and a composting toilet, which, honestly, I don't know if I could ever get used to. I watched this whole documentary on tiny houses last week because of her. Some of them are built on trailers so you can just hook them up to a truck and drive away whenever you want. That sounds kind of nice, just being able to pack up your whole house and move to the mountains or something. I've always wanted to visit the Rockies. I saw a picture of Banff National Park once and the water in the lakes there is this insane, bright turquoise color because of the rock flour from the glaciers. It doesn't even look real. But um, yeah, so I guess the whole thing with my mood and energy, it's been going on for about five months now, since I started that extra certification.\n\nDoctor: If you were to stop the extra certification course, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Um, if I stopped the course? I don't know, maybe? I mean, I can't really just drop it now, I've already paid the tuition and bought all the textbooks. Have you seen how expensive textbooks are lately? It's ridiculous. I had to buy this one massive manual on advanced pipefitting techniques, and it cost like two hundred dollars. It's so heavy, I actually use it to prop open the door to my apartment when I'm bringing in groceries. My apartment building is so old, the doors are solid wood and they swing shut so fast if you don't wedge something in there. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got her hand caught in the lobby door once because she wasn't paying attention. She's this really sweet older lady who always smells like lavender and bakes these amazing snickerdoodle cookies. She brings me a plate of them every Christmas. Last year she put them in this really pretty vintage tin that had a picture of a snowy village on it. I actually kept the tin, I use it to store all my extra drill bits and screws now. It's perfectly sized for that. But um... yeah, I'm not sure if quitting the class would make a difference. I haven't really thought about stopping.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings started to interfere with your work or classes?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, actually. It's getting kind of bad. My instructors and my foreman, Greg—the guy with the radio voice I mentioned?—they've both had to chase me down lately because I'm leaving sketches unfinished and my work is just... slower than it should be. Actually, he told me he used to do voiceovers for local car dealership commercials back in the day. You know those ones where they just yell about Sunday blowout sales? My dad used to absolutely hate those commercials. He'd mute the TV every single time one came on. We're learning all about greywater systems and how to reuse water from sinks and showers to flush toilets and water gardens. It's wild how much clean water we just flush down the drain every single day. My instructor for the course, she's really passionate about it. She actually lives in this tiny house she built herself, completely off the grid. She has solar panels and a composting toilet, which, honestly, I don't know if I could ever get used to. I watched this whole documentary on tiny houses last week because of her. Some of them are built on trailers so you can just hook them up to a truck and drive away whenever you want. That sounds kind of nice, just being able to pack up your whole house and move to the mountains or something. I've always wanted to visit the Rockies. I thought about dyeing my hair pink once, but I figured it would look weird with my hard hat. Plus, the dye stains everything. My friend Sarah dyed her hair blue in high school and her bathtub was permanently Smurf-colored for like three years. Her mom was so mad. They ended up having to re-glaze the whole tub before they sold the house. I even ignore the group texts for this volunteer thing I do. It's like I don't feel much of anything about them anymore, even though those were the things that made me feel like myself, you know? The volunteer group is actually really cool, we help build community gardens in empty lots around the city. You don't see it for a long time until the drywall gets all mushy and the paint starts bubbling. I had to fix a pipe like that last week in this really old apartment building downtown. It's this vintage John Deere that he refuses to get rid of, even though the carburetor gets flooded literally every other time he tries to cut the grass. I bought a plastic toolbox a few months ago for my apprenticeship and the main latch snapped off the second day I had it. It's just so frustrating when things aren't built to last anymore. But um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, feeling like this before. Yeah, no, this is definitely the first time I've ever felt this way."
